The distinction between intent-based and outcome-based justice, and the explicit efficiency bounds derived, can inform predictions about the likely efficiency gains or limits from punishment in related experimental or field PGGs, especially when considering the role of monitoring precision, transfer costs, and the possibility of multiple equilibria.

- source: Mihm, M., & Toth, R. (2020). Cooperative networks with robust private monitoring. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that punishment mechanisms can sustain efficient cooperation in networked repeated games, but the effectiveness of punishment—and thus the efficiency of equilibrium outcomes—depends critically on the monitoring structure. With perfect or partial monitoring (players can observe at least their neighbors' neighbors), punishment can be immediate and efficient networks are possible. With local (private) monitoring (players only observe their own ties), punishment is delayed and only highly clustered networks (with triadic closure) can robustly support cooperation. The paper provides formal incentive constraints (PIC, LIC, RIC) that characterize when efficient or near-efficient networks can be sustained. Efficiency is maximized when monitoring allows for rapid punishment, and is reduced when monitoring is local and punishment is delayed or less credible.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how the monitoring structure (analogous to 'show_other_summaries' and 'show_punishment_id'), network clustering, and the immediacy/effectiveness of punishment affect the efficiency of public-goods-like games. If punishment is possible and monitoring is sufficiently rich (players can observe others' actions beyond their own ties), then enabling punishment is likely to increase efficiency or at least sustain cooperation. If monitoring is strictly local, only highly clustered (triadically closed) networks can sustain cooperation, and efficiency gains from punishment are limited. The paper also shows that the cost and effectiveness of punishment (punishment_cost, punishment_tech) interact with network structure to determine whether efficient outcomes are possible. The results are most directly applicable to networked repeated games with bilateral ties, but the logic extends to public goods games with networked or local interaction structures.

- source: Nakao, K. (2009). CREATION OF SOCIAL ORDER IN ETHNIC CONFLICT. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of punishment mechanisms (peer monitoring, collective peer punishment, or ethnic conflict) can sustain higher group efficiency (expected payoffs) compared to no punishment, but only if in-group monitoring is sufficiently efficient and not too costly. When monitoring is inefficient or costly, or when noise (misinterpretation of actions) is high, punishment can reduce efficiency due to frequent erroneous punishment or high monitoring costs. The model provides comparative statics showing that efficiency is maximized with efficient peer monitoring and high tolerance, and that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by the quality of in-group policing, the length of conflict, and the density of inter-group interactions. The model predicts that groups with high-quality in-group policing achieve higher efficiency and more stable cooperation, while groups with low-quality policing suffer more frequent and longer conflicts, reducing efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated social dilemma games depends critically on the efficiency and cost of monitoring and punishment. If the game design allows for efficient, low-cost peer monitoring (low punishment cost, high punishment tech), enabling punishment increases efficiency relative to the control. If monitoring is costly or ineffective, punishment can reduce efficiency due to high costs or frequent erroneous punishment. The model also shows that the effect of punishment is moderated by noise (misinterpretation), group size, and the structure of interactions. For prediction, the paper suggests that enabling punishment will increase efficiency only when monitoring is efficient and not too costly, and that the marginal effect of punishment is lower or negative when monitoring is costly or noise is high. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, so findings should be transferred with caution.

- source: Jordan, J. J., & Rand, D. G. (2017). Third-party punishment as a costly signal of high continuation probabilities in repeated games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: This paper develops a formal model in which third-party punishment (TPP) acts as a costly signal of trustworthiness in repeated games. The model shows that when individuals differ in their probability of future interaction (continuation probability), only those with high continuation probability (trustworthy types) find it worthwhile to engage in TPP, as they are more likely to receive future reciprocal benefits. The signaling value of TPP can make punishment equilibrium behavior in parameter regions where, absent signaling, punishment would not occur. The model provides explicit payoff formulas and equilibrium conditions, and demonstrates via evolutionary simulations that the punishment signaling equilibrium is dynamically stable. The presence of TPP as a signal increases the set of parameter values for which cooperation and higher group payoffs are sustained, thus improving efficiency relative to the no-punishment baseline.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the availability of punishment as a costly signal can increase group efficiency in repeated games, especially when individuals differ in continuation probability and when signaling is possible. The model gives explicit conditions (in terms of cost/benefit ratios, continuation probabilities, and punishment costs) under which punishment increases efficiency. While the model is not a direct PGG or lab experiment, its structure is closely related to repeated social dilemma games and provides mechanistic insight into when and why punishment will improve efficiency. The findings suggest that, in environments where signaling is possible and individuals can benefit from future interactions, enabling punishment can lead to higher efficiency than disabling it, provided the cost of punishment is not too high.

- source: Balmaceda, F., & Escobar, J. F. (2017). Trust in cohesive communities. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency and welfare effects of punishment in repeated trust games depend critically on the structure of the social network and the speed of information flow. When information about defections spreads only to direct neighbors (slow information), cohesive networks (complete components) are optimal for sustaining cooperation and maximizing welfare, as they allow for coordinated punishment. When information spreads rapidly to all direct and indirect connections (fast information), minimally connected networks (e.g., trees) are optimal, as they maximize the number of players who can punish a defection. The paper also shows that the convexity or concavity of the welfare function determines whether stars or equally sized components maximize welfare. The analysis provides explicit characterizations of equilibrium payoffs and welfare under different network and monitoring regimes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance on how network structure and information transmission technology moderate the efficiency gains from punishment in repeated trust-like games. It shows that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not uniform but depends on whether the network allows for coordinated punishment and how quickly information about defections spreads. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that game design dimensions related to network structure, monitoring technology, and information flow are critical moderators of punishment's efficiency effects. However, the model is not a standard public goods game and does not provide empirical effect sizes, so quantitative predictions should be made with caution and only in settings structurally similar to the model.

- source: Stahl, D. O. (2011). Cooperation in the sporadically repeated prisoners' dilemma via reputation mechanisms. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in the SRPD, only a small subset of reputation mechanisms can sustain cooperation and achieve efficient outcomes. Specifically, mechanisms that distinguish between justified punishment and defection, and allow for penitence, can support equilibria where the long-run group payoff matches the fully cooperative outcome. The analysis shows that, with these mechanisms and high enough continuation probability, the population evolves toward full cooperation and efficiency. In contrast, most reputation mechanisms result in universal defection and zero efficiency. The paper also extends the analysis to clan-based (group) settings, showing that the efficiency result generalizes when within-group matching is sufficiently likely.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that the structure of the punishment/reputation mechanism is a critical moderator of the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated social dilemmas. If the punishment mechanism is designed to avoid penalizing justified punishers and allows for penitence, and if the continuation probability is high, then enabling punishment can move efficiency from zero (universal defection) to the full-cooperation benchmark. The findings suggest that, for prediction tasks, the details of the punishment/reputation update rule and the effective 'delta' (continuation probability) are crucial for forecasting the efficiency impact of punishment. The results are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, anonymous-matching games with public reputations, but the logic may transfer to PGG-like settings with similar reputation mechanisms.

- source: Corriveau, L. (2012). Game theory and the kula. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper demonstrates that in a repeated, circular n-player game (the 'game of kula'), the unique Nash equilibrium of the one-shot game is inefficient (all defect). However, when the game is repeated indefinitely and players are sufficiently patient, efficient (fully cooperative) equilibria can be sustained by social norms that attach punishment to deviations. The model formalizes three types of punishment-based norms: direct reciprocity (punished by the harmed party), indirect reciprocity (punished by a third party), and mixed reciprocity (punished by both). The mixed norm is shown to be the most robust, existing under the widest range of parameters. The analysis is entirely theoretical, but it provides formal conditions under which punishment enables efficient cooperation, and shows that the presence of punishment strategies can transform an inefficient game into an efficient one.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that enabling punishment in repeated, multi-player social dilemma games (with circular or networked structure) can increase efficiency, provided players are sufficiently patient and punishment is credible. The model is not a standard public goods game but is closely related, and the results generalize the repeated prisoner's dilemma. The findings suggest that, in environments where punishment is possible and observable, and where players have high discount factors, the introduction of punishment mechanisms can shift the equilibrium from inefficient (defection) to efficient (cooperation), thus increasing group payoff. The paper also clarifies that the structure of punishment (direct, indirect, mixed) affects the robustness of cooperation, with mixed norms being most robust. For prediction tasks, this paper supports a positive effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated, networked social dilemmas, especially as the number of players increases and as the possibility of both direct and indirect punishment exists.

- source: Murase, Y. (2025). Costly punishment sustains indirect reciprocity under low defection detectability. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of costly punishment in sustaining cooperation depends critically on the detectability of defection. When defection is difficult to detect (i.e., high probability that defection is misperceived as cooperation), social norms that include costly punishment (CP norms) can be the most efficient ESS, achieving high cooperation and high average payoff. When defection is easy to detect, punishment is rarely efficient and often reduces payoffs. The model provides explicit boundaries for when punishment is efficient, and shows that punishment is most effective when it is rarely executed but strongly deters defection. Execution errors and high punishment costs shrink the parameter space where punishment is efficient. The results challenge previous theoretical work that found only a narrow efficiency margin for punishment, showing that under asymmetric detection errors, punishment can be broadly efficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, parameterized theoretical predictions for the effect of punishment on efficiency in indirect reciprocity games with pairwise donation structure. While not a standard public goods game, the model is adjacent and the efficiency results are highly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment in environments where defection is hard to detect. The explicit formulas and phase diagrams allow mapping from game design dimensions (punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, error rates) and baseline efficiency to the expected efficiency with punishment enabled. The main moderator is the detectability of defection: when it is low, punishment can substantially increase efficiency; when high, punishment is unnecessary or even harmful. The results are most transferable to settings with pairwise interactions, reputation, and indirect reciprocity, but provide strong mechanistic insight for broader PGG-like environments.

- source: Camera, G., & Gioffré, A. (2014). A tractable analysis of contagious equilibria. *Journal of Mathematical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that contagious punishment (grim trigger strategies) in repeated random-matching social dilemmas can sustain full cooperation (i.e., maximal efficiency) if players are sufficiently patient (high discount factor), the cost of cooperation is not too high, and monitoring is sufficiently broad. The authors derive closed-form expressions for continuation payoffs and provide exact bounds on the discount factor and the cost of cooperation required to make cooperation self-enforcing. They show that increasing the breadth of monitoring (i.e., allowing players to observe more actions outside their own match) makes it easier to sustain cooperation, lowering the required discount factor and cost of cooperation. Conversely, if defection is not absorbing (i.e., if players can revert to cooperation), sustaining cooperation becomes harder. The results are general for random-matching repeated games with contagious punishment, and the framework is intended to inform both theory and laboratory design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the presence and structure of punishment (specifically, contagious, unforgiving punishment) can enable high efficiency in repeated social dilemmas, even with random matching and anonymity. The key moderators are the discount factor, the cost of cooperation, and the breadth of monitoring (how many others' actions are observed). The results suggest that, in environments with strong punishment and sufficient monitoring, enabling punishment should increase efficiency relative to control (no punishment), provided the other parameters (patience, cost) are favorable. The paper also provides explicit formulas for the parameter thresholds needed to sustain cooperation, which could be used to predict when punishment will be effective in raising efficiency. However, the results are theoretical and apply to repeated Prisoner's Dilemma-type games, not public goods games per se, though the logic is closely related.

- source: Jindani, S. (2020). Community enforcement using modal actions. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper proves that in repeated random matching games where players are matched with multiple partners and can observe modal actions, any efficient (strictly individually rational) payoff can be supported as a strict, sequential equilibrium. This includes full cooperation in the prisoner's dilemma and similar games. The mechanism relies on community punishment: players who deviate (i.e., are non-modal) are sanctioned by others, and those who fail to sanction are also sanctioned. The equilibrium is robust to trembles and the presence of noisy players, and the efficiency loss due to noise is linear in the noise rate. However, the mechanism is not robust to observation errors unless these are rare relative to other noise. The informational burden is low: in the prisoner's dilemma, only one period of memory is needed to sustain cooperation. The results generalize to a wide class of games and show that community enforcement with punishment can achieve high efficiency under realistic informational constraints.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that, in repeated random matching games with multiple partners per period and observable modal actions, enabling community punishment can raise efficiency to the full cooperative level, provided players are sufficiently patient and the population is large. The results are robust to some forms of noise, and the informational requirements are minimal. For prediction tasks, the presence of multiple matching, observable modal actions, and community punishment mechanisms should be expected to enable high efficiency, even in large groups. However, if observation errors are frequent or if players cannot observe modal actions, efficiency may not be sustained. The findings are most directly applicable to settings structurally similar to the model (random matching, multiple partners, observable actions, and community punishment), and less so to standard public goods games with fixed groups and no community enforcement.

- source: Okamoto, K., & Matsumura, S. (2000). The evolution of punishment and apology: an iterated prisoner's dilemma model. *Evolutionary Ecology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that strategies incorporating both punishment and apology signals (especially the Pavlov-like pPAS strategy) can maintain high levels of cooperation and average payoffs in the iterated prisoner's dilemma, even in the presence of errors and deceptive strategies. The average payoff in a pPAS-dominated population is nearly as high as in a fully cooperative population, and much higher than in populations dominated by tit-for-tat (TFT) or defectors. The success of pPAS depends on the costs of punishment and apology: pPAS dominates when the cost of giving punishment is low, the cost of receiving punishment is high, or the cost of giving apology is high. The model also shows that pPAS can remain stable even when there is a dominance asymmetry in costs between players. The findings suggest that punishment and apology mechanisms can evolve to support efficient cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated social dilemmas with error, the availability of punishment (and apology) mechanisms can substantially increase group efficiency, provided the costs of punishment and apology are within certain bounds. The results are most directly applicable to iterated prisoner's dilemma games, which are adjacent but not identical to public goods games. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency, one should expect a positive effect if the punishment cost is not too high, the punishment is effective, and the apology cost is not too low (to prevent deception). The paper also highlights the importance of considering the cost structure of punishment and apology when making such predictions. However, the results are based on theoretical models and evolutionary simulations, not empirical experiments.

- source: Leimar, O. (1997). Repeated games: A state space approach. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated games like the IPD and dominance games, the ability to punish can support the evolutionary stability of cooperative strategies, leading to higher efficiency (i.e., higher expected payoffs, closer to full cooperation) when the probability of continuation is high and punishment is sufficiently severe and/or effective. However, the effect of punishment on efficiency is parameter-dependent: if the probability of continuation is too low or punishment is too costly/ineffective, cooperation cannot be sustained and efficiency is low. The analysis also shows that there are many possible stable strategies, some of which are more efficient than others, and that the structure of punishment (e.g., how many rounds of mutual defection follow a defection) affects the range of stability and efficiency. In dominance games, punishment by a dominant can enforce resource control and higher efficiency for the dominant, but alternative equilibria exist where the subdominant prevails. In alternating PD, reciprocal strategies can be stable and efficient if the benefit-to-cost ratio is high enough. The findings are theoretical and based on formal analysis of payoffs and stability conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated games depends on game design dimensions such as the probability of continuation (analogous to number of rounds), the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the structure of the game (simultaneous vs. alternating moves, role asymmetry). It shows that punishment can increase efficiency by supporting cooperation, but only when parameters are favorable (e.g., high continuation probability, effective punishment). The results suggest that, for prediction tasks, one should expect punishment to improve efficiency in repeated games when the game is long enough and punishment is not too costly or weak, but that the effect can be neutral or negative if these conditions are not met. The paper also highlights that many different punishment strategies can be stable, so the specific implementation of punishment matters. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency in repeated, two-player, payoff-based games with punishment.

- source: Laclau, M., & Tomala, T. (2017). Repeated games with public deterministic monitoring. *Journal of Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, reward_exists, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper provides a general theoretical framework for understanding how punishment and reward mechanisms can be used to sustain efficient outcomes in repeated games with public monitoring. It shows that the set of sustainable payoffs is characterized by admissibility and joint rationality, and that explicit punishment phases (using approachability) can deter deviations. However, the efficiency achievable in equilibrium is limited by the monitoring structure: if deviations are not detectable or not attributable, efficiency may be strictly less than the social optimum. The paper gives explicit constructions of equilibrium strategies with main (cooperation), punishment, compensation, and reward phases, and proves that for sufficiently patient players, any payoff in the interior of the admissible and jointly rational set can be supported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the efficiency of repeated games with punishment, especially in settings with imperfect public monitoring. It provides a theoretical upper bound on efficiency as a function of the monitoring structure and the ability to implement punishment phases. The explicit construction of punishment and reward phases shows how punishment can be used to sustain cooperation, but also highlights that efficiency is limited by the possibility of undetectable or unattributable deviations. For the prediction task, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends critically on the monitoring structure: when monitoring is sufficiently informative, punishment can support high efficiency; when it is not, efficiency is bounded away from the optimum. The results are general and apply to a wide class of repeated games, including but not limited to public goods games.

- source: Olcina, G., & Calabuig, V. (2015). COORDINATED PUNISHMENT AND THE EVOLUTION OF COOPERATION. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the long-run efficiency of a team trust game with coordinated punishment depends critically on the institutional parameters of punishment: the effectiveness of coordinated punishment (damage inflicted if enough punishers act), the cost of punishment, and the strength of peer punishment (peer pressure on non-punishers). If the effectiveness of coordinated punishment is high enough (damage parameter λ ≥ 0.5) and peer pressure is strong (peer punishment cost to defectors is high relative to cost of coordinated punishment), then the only stable long-run outcome is a fully cooperative, efficient culture with no observed punishment (because the threat is credible). If these conditions are not met, the system converges to a non-cooperative, inefficient culture. The model also shows that increasing the effectiveness of punishment, lowering its cost, or increasing peer pressure all make the efficient outcome more likely. The results are robust to the dynamics of preference transmission, and the model provides explicit boundaries for the basins of attraction of efficient vs inefficient cultures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling effective, coordinated punishment (with sufficient effectiveness and/or low enough cost) and strong peer punishment will increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, even when the baseline (no-punishment) efficiency is low. The model is not a standard PGG but is closely related, and the mechanisms (threshold punishment, peer punishment, endogenous preference evolution) are highly relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. The explicit parameter boundaries (e.g., λ ≥ 0.5 for effectiveness, peer punishment cost vs coordinated punishment cost) can inform quantitative predictions about when punishment will be effective. The model also highlights that if punishment is not sufficiently effective or peer pressure is weak, enabling punishment may not improve efficiency. The results are most directly applicable to trust/investment games with team-based punishment, but the logic is transferable to PGG variants with threshold or coordinated punishment.

- source: Nakamaru, M., Shimura, H., Kitakaji, Y., & Ohnuma, S. (2018). The effect of sanctions on the evolution of cooperation in linear division of labor. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing sanctions—especially the producer responsibility system—promotes the evolution of cooperation and increases group-level efficiency in a linear division of labor game. The actor responsibility system only promotes cooperation if monitoring and detection are sufficiently effective, which is often not the case in real-world settings. The producer responsibility system, which sanctions the generator if the manifest is not returned, is robust to low monitoring probabilities and is more effective at promoting cooperation and reducing illegal dumping. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation is stable and shows that the three-role model captures more realistic dynamics than simpler two-role models. The findings are supported by both analytical results and numerical simulations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that introducing a well-designed punishment system (especially one that does not rely on costly monitoring, like the producer responsibility system) will increase group efficiency in public-goods-like environments with linear division of labor. The model is adjacent to standard PGGs but is directly relevant for settings with sequential roles and externalities. The results suggest that, for games with similar structure, enabling punishment (especially with high effectiveness and low monitoring requirements) should be predicted to increase efficiency relative to control. The paper also highlights the importance of the effectiveness of monitoring and the structure of the sanction system as moderators. However, the model is theoretical and does not provide direct empirical estimates of effect sizes.

- source: Yu, T. K., Chen, S. H., & Li, H. G. (2016). Social norms, costly punishment and the evolution of cooperation. *Journal of Economic Interaction and Coordination*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper demonstrates that allowing or encouraging costly punishment in a donor-recipient game increases both the size of the basin of attraction for the cooperative evolutionary stable state (CESS) and the speed at which a society converges to this high-payoff state. Under parameter settings where punishment is not too costly, the presence of punishment options increases the probability that a society will escape the non-cooperative trap and reach high efficiency. The effect is stronger when the social norm actively encourages punishment. However, in the long run, after full cooperation is achieved, continued costly punishment can reduce efficiency due to unnecessary punishment costs. The findings are based on mathematical modeling and numerical simulations, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in repeated social dilemma environments with reputation and norm dynamics, enabling costly punishment increases the likelihood and speed of reaching high-efficiency cooperative states, especially when the social norm encourages punishment. The effect is most pronounced in 'developing' societies or initial conditions with many defectors. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment (especially with supportive norms) will generally increase efficiency relative to control, unless punishment is extremely costly. However, in fully cooperative steady states, punishment may reduce efficiency due to its cost. The model is not a standard PGG but is structurally similar, so findings are likely transferable to PGG-like settings with reputation and norm evolution.

- source: Celik, G., & Peters, M. (2016). Reciprocal relationships and mechanism design. *Canadian Journal of Economics-revue Canadienne D Economique*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in environments where players cannot enforce punishments for non-cooperation, reciprocal contracting (where players can make commitments contingent on others' commitments) allows for a larger set of efficient outcomes than centralized mechanism design with the same punishment constraints. The key mechanism is that players can use contract offers to signal private information, which can lower the reservation utility of deviators and thus make more efficient outcomes individually rational and incentive compatible. The analysis is theoretical, with the Cournot duopoly serving as a worked example. The results show that, under certain conditions (private values, single crossing, transferable utility), the incentive compatibility constraints can be simplified, and efficient outcomes can be implemented with appropriate transfer schemes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that, even in the absence of enforceable punishment, mechanisms that allow for reciprocal commitments and information revelation can achieve higher efficiency than mechanisms that do not. For the downstream prediction task, the main implication is that the absence of punishment does not necessarily preclude efficient outcomes if the game design allows for reciprocal contracting and signaling. However, the results are derived in a general mechanism design context (e.g., Cournot duopoly, collusion, marriage, etc.) rather than in standard public goods games. The findings suggest that, in environments structurally similar to public goods games but with richer contracting and signaling possibilities, efficiency can be higher than predicted by models that only consider enforceable punishment or centralized mechanisms. The paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency changes when punishment is enabled or disabled in standard PGGs, but it does clarify the theoretical boundaries of what is possible in related environments.

- source: Povey, R. (2014). Punishment and the potency of group selection. *Journal of Evolutionary Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that embedding a punishment system within a group selection framework reduces the evolutionary success of altruism. While punishment can statically deter selfishness and improve immediate social welfare, it simultaneously weakens the group selection mechanism that would otherwise promote the evolution of intrinsic altruism. This leads to a lower equilibrium level of altruism and, in the long run, can reduce dynamic social welfare. The model shows that the requirements for group selection to sustain altruism become more stringent when punishment is available, and that the use of punishment may have negative dynamic welfare implications, even if it improves static outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling punishment in a group environment may increase static efficiency (short-term group payoff) but can reduce dynamic efficiency (long-run welfare) by undermining the evolution of intrinsic cooperation. The model is not a standard public goods game but is adjacent, focusing on sequential punishment and group selection. The findings imply that, in environments where intrinsic motivation is important for long-run efficiency, introducing punishment may have ambiguous or even negative effects on overall efficiency, especially over evolutionary or cultural timescales. The paper is most informative for settings where the persistence of cooperation and welfare over time is of interest, rather than immediate, one-shot efficiency gains.

- source: Larson, J. M. (2016). The evolutionary advantage of limited network knowledge. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in a repeated prisoner's dilemma with gossip-based punishment, the ability to sustain full cooperation (and thus maximize group efficiency/fitness) depends on both the structure of the social network and the precision of individuals' knowledge about that structure. When all individuals have precise knowledge of the network, it is harder to sustain full cooperation because some individuals can be identified as weakly connected and thus are tempting targets for defection. When individuals have only coarse knowledge (e.g., know only the average neighborhood size), it is easier to sustain full cooperation, as targeting is not possible. The minimum discount factor required to sustain cooperation is lower under coarse knowledge than under precise knowledge, making full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) more robust. The model thus predicts that limited network knowledge facilitates the enforcement of cooperation through punishment, leading to higher group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in sustaining high efficiency in repeated social dilemmas depends not only on the presence of punishment but also on the information structure—specifically, the precision of network knowledge. In environments where individuals have only coarse knowledge of the network, punishment is more effective at sustaining full cooperation and thus maximizing efficiency. If a public goods game or similar environment allows only limited information about the network or about others' positions, the model predicts a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency. Conversely, if players have precise knowledge of the network, the positive effect of punishment on efficiency may be reduced. The paper does not provide direct empirical estimates but offers clear theoretical mechanisms and comparative statics relevant to prediction.

- source: Vincent, J. R. (2007). Spatial dynamics, social norms, and the opportunity of the commons. *Ecological Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that economic incentives—specifically, the use of sanctions (punishments for non-cooperation and rewards for cooperation)—are critical for achieving efficient and sustainable management of common pool resources. Theoretical models (e.g., repeated games, evolutionary game theory) and empirical case studies (e.g., Ostrom 1990) show that monitoring and enforcement mechanisms enable groups to avoid the tragedy of the commons, leading to higher group profits and resource stocks. The author is skeptical that social norms alone, without sanctions, can achieve similar efficiency, especially when rapid or large-scale behavioral change is needed. The paper also notes that public goods (as opposed to common pool resources) face similar free-rider problems and require external enforcement (e.g., taxes, quotas) to achieve efficient provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment or sanctions in public-goods-game-like environments will increase efficiency, group payoff, and resource sustainability, especially when compared to environments without such mechanisms. The presence of monitoring and enforcement is highlighted as a key moderator: systems with sanctions are much more likely to achieve efficient outcomes than those relying on norms alone. The paper does not provide quantitative estimates but offers strong theoretical and qualitative support for the positive effect of punishment on efficiency. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment/sanctions should be coded as a strong positive moderator of efficiency, especially in settings analogous to common pool resources.

- source: Wei, X., Xu, P., Du, S. T., Yan, G. H., & Pei, H. Y. (2021). Reputational preference-based payoff punishment promotes cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that introducing a reputational preference-based payoff punishment mechanism in spatial social dilemmas (PDG and SDG) significantly increases the fraction of cooperators. At low punishment intensity, both cooperators and defectors may have low or negative average payoffs due to the cost of punishment, but as punishment intensity increases, cooperators' average payoff and fitness rise, and defectors' payoffs drop to zero. At high punishment intensity, the system can reach full cooperation with high average payoffs for cooperators. The effect is more pronounced in the snowdrift game, where coexistence of cooperators and defectors persists over a wider parameter range. The study does not directly report group-level efficiency, but the increase in average payoffs for cooperators at high punishment intensity suggests a positive effect on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical/simulation evidence that introducing a costly, reputation-based punishment mechanism in spatial social dilemmas increases cooperation rates and, at sufficient punishment intensity, increases average payoffs for cooperators, leading to higher group efficiency. The effect is robust across both prisoner's dilemma and snowdrift games, with stronger effects in the latter. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling punishment (especially with high effectiveness and shared cost) will increase efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition, particularly in spatially structured games. However, the results are based on simulation, not empirical data, and do not use the public goods game directly, so transfer to PGG-like environments should be cautious but is likely informative.

- source: Rand, D. G., Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2009). Direct reciprocity with costly punishment: Generous tit-for-tat prevails. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper shows that in repeated Prisoner's Dilemma games with costly punishment, the addition of a punishment option does not increase cooperation or group payoffs in evolutionary simulations. Even when Nash equilibrium analysis allows for cooperative equilibria using punishment, evolutionary selection in finite populations favors generous tit-for-tat strategies that do not use costly punishment. Costly punishment is rarely used, and when it is, it is associated with lower payoffs. The presence of costly punishment does not allow cooperation to evolve in parameter regions where it would not already be supported by direct reciprocity alone. These findings are robust to variations in game parameters and are supported by experimental data from Dreber et al. (2008).
  prediction_guidance: For the prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling costly punishment in a repeated two-player PD-like environment does not increase efficiency or group payoff, regardless of the punishment cost or effectiveness. Evolutionary dynamics select against strategies that use costly punishment, and the most successful strategies are those that reciprocate cooperation and defect against defectors (generous tit-for-tat), not those that punish. Thus, when predicting the effect of enabling punishment in similar repeated games, one should not expect an increase in efficiency or group payoff, and may even expect a negative association between punishment use and payoff. However, the results are specific to repeated two-player PDs and may not generalize to larger group public goods games.

- source: Hu, B., & Du, Y. X. (2023). Integrated analysis of employee cooperation and conflict behaviors in the context of digital technology. *Simulation-transactions of the Society for Modeling and Simulation International*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the presence and magnitude of punishment (penalty for conflict) is a key determinant of stable cooperation in a group setting modeled as an evolutionary game. If the penalty for conflict exceeds half the cost of cooperation, cooperation becomes the evolutionary stable strategy, and the group achieves higher average perceived payoffs. If the penalty is lower, the group exhibits unstable, turbulent behavior with frequent reversals between cooperation and conflict, and the average perceived payoff from cooperation is often lower than from conflict. The model also shows that increasing the proportion of cooperators and the perceived benefit of cooperation stabilizes group behavior. The findings are based on theoretical analysis and simulation, not on real-world or laboratory data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that increasing the penalty for non-cooperation (punishment) above a critical threshold (half the cost of cooperation) can shift group behavior from unstable, low-payoff states to stable, high-cooperation, higher-payoff states. For prediction tasks, the presence and magnitude of punishment cost and effectiveness are critical moderators. However, the outcomes are modeled as perceived payoffs and behavioral states, not as direct group efficiency ratios. The findings are most relevant for settings where punishment is a salient, well-understood, and enforceable mechanism, and less so for empirical prediction of actual group efficiency in experimental PGGs.

- source: Cui, P. B., & Wu, Z. X. (2014). Selfish punishment with avoiding mechanism can alleviate both first-order and second-order social dilemma. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that introducing selfish punishment with an avoiding mechanism into a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can alleviate both first-order and second-order social dilemmas, depending on the punishment cost, fine, and identification probability. The presence of selfish punishers allows for the stable coexistence of cooperators and punishers, and under certain parameter regimes, reduces the prevalence of defectors and second-order free riders. Theoretical predictions using extended pair approximation and interaction web analysis match well with simulation results. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy type, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment (especially when including mechanisms for selfish punishment and avoidance) can promote cooperation and reduce free riding in spatially structured social dilemmas. However, because the model is a prisoner's dilemma (not a public goods game) and the main outcomes are strategy frequencies rather than group efficiency or total payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that the effectiveness of punishment depends on the cost/fine ratio and the ability to identify defectors, but do not provide direct quantitative predictions for efficiency outcomes in public goods games. The paper is most useful for understanding mechanisms and qualitative moderators of punishment effects, rather than for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Gao, S. P., Wu, T., Nie, S. L., & Wang, L. (2015). Promote or hinder? The role of punishment in the emergence of cooperation. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation depends critically on the pattern of punishment and the presence of anti-social punishment. Centralized (pool) pro-social punishment is more effective than decentralized (peer) punishment at promoting cooperation. When anti-social punishment is possible and both pro- and anti-social punishments are in the same pattern, the positive effect of punishment is largely neutralized. However, if pro-social punishment is centralized and anti-social punishment is decentralized, cooperation can still be promoted. The results are derived from theoretical evolutionary game models and focus on the stationary abundance of strategies, not direct efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative theoretical evidence about how punishment design (centralized vs decentralized, presence of anti-social punishment) can affect the evolutionary success of cooperation in social dilemmas. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that centralized punishment mechanisms are more robust to anti-social punishment and more likely to sustain cooperation. For prediction tasks, this suggests that games with centralized punishment (pool) are more likely to see positive effects of punishment on efficiency, especially when anti-social punishment is possible. However, the results are based on evolutionary dynamics and strategy abundance, not direct efficiency measures, and the model is a prisoner's dilemma rather than a public goods game.

- source: Sánchez, A., & Cuesta, J. A. (2005). Altruism may arise from individual selection. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that strong reciprocity (altruistic punishment) can arise and persist in agent-based evolutionary models of the Ultimatum Game under individual selection, without the need for group or cultural selection, repeated interactions, or reputation. The key mechanism is the role of fluctuations and finite selection intervals, which allow altruistic punishers to survive and proliferate. The model reproduces the empirical finding that offers and acceptance thresholds are much higher than the self-interested Nash equilibrium. However, the paper does not analyze or report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, focusing instead on the distribution and evolution of behavioral strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the possibility that punishment (in the form of rejection of unfair offers) can evolve and persist under individual selection in one-shot, anonymous games. However, it does not provide evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. The findings are most relevant for understanding the evolutionary plausibility of punishment behaviors, not for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGGs with or without punishment. Thus, its direct value for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions is limited, though it may inform mechanistic expectations about the robustness of punishment behaviors.

- source: Fang, X., & Chen, X. J. (2021). Evolutionary dynamics of trust in the N-player trust game with individual reward and punishment. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The introduction of individual reward and punishment in the N-player trust game can promote the stable coexistence of investors and trustworthy trustees, which is interpreted as a promotion of trust. The size of the attraction domain for this coexistence increases with both the overall incentive strength and the share of incentives devoted to reward (as opposed to punishment). Reward is found to be more effective than punishment in promoting trust. The analysis is theoretical, using replicator dynamics, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the results imply that mechanisms increasing trust and trustworthiness can move the system toward more socially optimal states.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect theoretical support for the idea that introducing punishment (and especially reward) mechanisms can promote cooperation-like behavior in trust-based social dilemmas. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report quantitative efficiency outcomes for control vs. punishment-enabled conditions. The model is adjacent to public goods games, so its findings may be cautiously extrapolated to similar environments, especially regarding the positive effect of incentive strength and the relative effectiveness of reward over punishment. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that increasing the strength and effectiveness of incentives (especially reward) is likely to expand the basin of attraction for cooperative outcomes, but does not provide direct efficiency data.

- source: Christoforou, E., Anta, A. F., Georgiou, C., Mosteiro, M. A., & Sánchez, A. (2013). Crowd Computing as a Cooperation Problem: An Evolutionary Approach. *Journal of Statistical Physics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that introducing punishment for cheating in a repeated master-worker (principal-agent) setting with reinforcement learning leads to faster convergence to a state where all workers cooperate (i.e., provide correct answers), compared to no punishment. However, punishment is not strictly necessary for eventual correctness if other conditions (such as sufficient reward relative to aspiration and cost) are met. The system is robust to high tolerance for defectors, but as group size increases, high tolerance can reduce the probability of achieving full cooperation. The main outcomes are the probability of cheating and the system's convergence to correctness, not direct measures of group efficiency or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it shows that punishment reduces the average cheating probability and accelerates convergence to a fully cooperative state. In settings where efficiency is closely tied to the rate of cooperation (e.g., if only correct answers generate group benefit), these results suggest that punishment would increase efficiency, especially in the short-to-medium term. However, the absence of explicit payoff or efficiency data limits the direct applicability of these findings to the prediction task. The effects of group size, tolerance, and aspiration are also informative: higher group size and higher tolerance to defectors can undermine the effectiveness of punishment in achieving full cooperation.

- source: Chang, Q., & Zhang, Y. (2021). Peer pressure in extortion game can resolve social dilemma. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing peer pressure (punishment) into a spatial donation/extortion game can promote the prevalence of cooperation, especially at moderate punishment intensities. When punishment is too strong, extortioners dominate. The effect of punishment is non-monotonic: moderate punishment helps cooperation, but high punishment harms it. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy, not group payoff or efficiency. The findings are based on simulation, not empirical data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic evidence that punishment can promote cooperation in spatial evolutionary games with extortion strategies, but only within a certain range of punishment intensity. However, because the outcomes are frequencies of strategies rather than group payoff or efficiency, and because the model is a spatial donation/extortion game (not a standard public goods game), the results are only adjacent to the prediction task. The paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games, but it does suggest that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, possibly efficiency) is non-monotonic and context-dependent.

- source: Zhang, C. Y., Zhu, Y. Y., Chen, Z. Q., & Zhang, J. L. (2017). Punishment in the form of shared cost promotes altruism in the cooperative dilemma games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a multi-player Prisoner's Dilemma with punishment (where only cooperators punish and share the cost), the evolutionary outcome depends on the punishment parameters and group size. Higher punishment effectiveness (greater penalty to defectors) and lower cost to punishers promote the spread and fixation of cooperation. Larger group size and higher temptation to defect make cooperation harder to sustain. The model provides explicit conditions under which cooperation can dominate, coexist with defection, or be eliminated. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that punishment with shared cost can promote the fixation of cooperation under certain parameter regimes, especially when punishment is effective and not too costly. The effect of group size (player_count) and temptation to defect (analogous to MPCR) are also analyzed. The results suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment with shared cost is likely to increase cooperation rates (and thus efficiency) when punishment is strong and not too costly, but the effect is weaker or negative when punishment is weak or costly, or when group size is large. However, because the model outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency, the translation to efficiency must be inferred.

- source: Sekiguchi, T., & Nakamaru, M. (2009). Effect of the presence of empty sites on the evolution of cooperation by costly punishment in spatial games. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The presence of empty sites in spatially structured populations (lattices) promotes the evolution of cooperation and does not reduce the effect of costly punishment. In contrast, in completely mixing populations, empty sites reduce the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation. The evolutionary success of strategies depends on the benefit of cooperation, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the density of empty sites. The model does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but rather which strategies dominate under different parameter regimes. The findings suggest that spatial structure and population density (via empty sites) are important moderators of the evolutionary dynamics of punishment and cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It shows that spatial structure (analogous to networked or local interaction in PGGs) and the presence of empty sites (analogous to non-participation or dropout) can strongly moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation. In spatial settings, punishment remains effective even with empty sites, while in well-mixed settings, empty sites undermine punishment's effectiveness. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results are most useful for understanding when punishment is likely to promote cooperation (and by extension, possibly efficiency) depending on spatial structure and participation rates, but do not provide quantitative efficiency effects.

- source: Liu, W. W., & Yang, J. N. (2018). The Evolutionary Game Theoretic Analysis for Sustainable Cooperation Relationship of Collaborative Innovation Network in Strategic Emerging Industries. *Sustainability*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that government intervention through incentives and punishments can significantly influence the frequency of positive cooperation between enterprises and URIs in collaborative innovation networks. When the sum of incentives and punishments exceeds the total additional cost (TAC) of cooperation, positive cooperation becomes stable. If incentives and punishments are too low, negative cooperation prevails. The model does not report group payoffs, efficiency, or welfare, but focuses on the conditions for stable cooperation strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that punishment (and incentives) can increase the frequency of cooperative behavior in a multi-agent innovation network, but it does not quantify effects on efficiency or group payoff. The model is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves free-riding and cooperation, but the outcomes are behavioral (strategy frequencies) rather than payoff-based. The findings suggest that, in similar environments, increasing punishment and incentives above a threshold can shift the system toward more cooperation, which may imply higher efficiency, but this is not directly modeled or measured.

- source: Chowdhury, S. N., Kundu, S., Banerjee, J., Perc, M., & Ghosh, D. (2021). Eco-evolutionary dynamics of cooperation in the presence of policing. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that the introduction of punishment (policing) as a third strategy in a repeated PD game can lead to stable coexistence or oscillatory dominance cycles among cooperators, defectors, and punishers, depending on ecological and game parameters. The strength of punishment, temptation to defect, and ecological benefits (from 'free space') all modulate which strategies persist. Moderate punishment can suppress defectors and promote cooperation, but high temptation to defect can still undermine cooperation. The model also demonstrates that initial conditions and parameter choices can lead to multistability, with different attractors (e.g., dominance by one strategy, coexistence, or extinction). The focus is on the eco-evolutionary dynamics of strategy frequencies, not on group efficiency or total welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that punishment can promote the persistence of cooperation in social dilemma settings, but the results are framed in terms of population dynamics (strategy frequencies and coexistence) rather than efficiency or group payoff. The model is based on a repeated PD game, not a public goods game, and does not report efficiency as defined by total group payoff relative to full cooperation. Therefore, while the findings support the idea that punishment can help sustain cooperation under certain conditions, they do not provide direct quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency changes in PGG-like environments with or without punishment. The results may be useful for understanding qualitative moderators (e.g., the importance of punishment strength, temptation, ecological context), but are not directly transferable to the efficiency prediction task.

- source: Gavrilets, S. (2020). The dynamics of injunctive social norms. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that the presence of punishment (active disapproval) increases the stability and prevalence of norm compliance, can create multiple stable equilibria, and increases the complexity of group behavioral dynamics. Heterogeneity in individual values and costs leads to diverse group outcomes. Punishment stabilizes cooperative equilibria and is mainly administered by those with high norm internalization. The models are general and can be applied to a range of social norm contexts, including public-goods-like settings, but do not directly model or report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that punishment mechanisms can stabilize norm compliance and cooperative behavior, especially in heterogeneous populations. However, it does not provide quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, nor does it report empirical data. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence and cost of punishment, as well as heterogeneity in values and beliefs, are important moderators of the effect of punishment on group behavior. The models are adjacent to public goods games but do not map directly onto the efficiency prediction task.

- source: Bosworth, W. (2022). Social Power and Non-cooperative Game Theory. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper argues that the credibility of threats and offers (punishments and rewards) is central to understanding social power and governance in non-cooperative games. It shows that resources, reputation, and coordination problems determine the ability to credibly punish or reward, and thus to exercise power. The analysis highlights the importance of commitment mechanisms and tipping points in reputation for sustaining credible threats. However, the paper does not provide empirical findings or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical insights into how punishment and reward mechanisms may affect cooperation and social power in collective action settings, emphasizing the importance of credibility, resources, and coordination. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its value for prediction is limited to high-level mechanism discussion and may inform the interpretation of empirical results from other sources.

- source: Toelstede, B. (2020). The Absence of Policing and the Political Economy of Contribution and Defection. *Democracy & Security*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in large groups, the disappearance of institutional punishment leads to a delayed decline in public goods contribution and a rapid increase in defection, while the reappearance of punishment leads to a delayed recovery of contribution and a rapid decline in defection. The length of these delays depends on factors such as trust in the punisher, transparency, media behavior, and regime support. The paper highlights the asymmetry between small and large groups, and between peer and institutional punishment, in terms of the speed and nature of behavioral responses. It also discusses the role of blame assignment and political manipulation in shaping these dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide quantitative or directly predictive evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. However, it offers theoretical and qualitative insights into how the effect of punishment on contribution (and by extension, efficiency) may be moderated by group size, institutional structure, trust, and information transparency. For prediction tasks, the main implication is that in large, real-world groups, the positive effect of punishment on contribution (and thus efficiency) may be delayed or attenuated compared to small, transparent, lab-like settings. The paper is not suitable for direct quantitative prediction but may inform qualitative expectations about moderators.

- source: López-Pérez, R. (2008). Aversion to norm-breaking:: A model. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper develops a formal model in which agents internalize social norms (specifically, an 'E-norm' combining efficiency and equality) and experience emotional costs (shame, anger) when violating or observing violations of these norms. The model predicts that people will cooperate and punish norm violators even at a cost to themselves, and that punishment is targeted at those responsible for norm violations. The model is shown to qualitatively account for a range of experimental findings in games such as dictator, ultimatum, trust, centipede, Cournot, and public goods games. In the context of public goods games, the model predicts that the introduction of punishment opportunities increases cooperation by deterring selfish behavior, as selfish agents comply to avoid being punished by principled agents. However, the paper does not provide direct quantitative predictions or empirical estimates of efficiency or group payoff with and without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a theoretical rationale for why punishment increases cooperation and norm compliance in public goods and related games, suggesting that the presence of punishment opportunities should increase efficiency relative to control conditions without punishment, especially when a sufficient proportion of players are 'principled' and willing to punish norm violations. However, the model is qualitative and does not specify the magnitude of efficiency gains or how they depend on specific game design dimensions (e.g., player count, punishment cost, MPCR). The model does not provide direct evidence for predicting treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game parameters, but supports the general expectation that punishment opportunities will have a more positive effect on efficiency in environments where norm internalization and willingness to punish are present.

- source: Ziegler, R. (1997). The normative structure of solidarity and inequality. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in both dyadic and collective solidarity games (including public-goods-like settings), strategies that combine defection with active sanctioning of others (Hypocritical Cooperation) can robustly enforce high levels of solidarity (cooperation), provided the costs of sanctioning are not too high. However, this can also lead to 'overcompliance' or excessive norm enforcement unless checked by counterstrategies (Full Opposition). The evolutionary outcome depends on the mix of strategies, the costs and effectiveness of sanctions, and the distribution of 'poor' and 'rich' in the population. The model does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but suggests that informal punishment can sustain cooperation under certain conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the idea that informal punishment mechanisms can sustain cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially when the cost of sanctioning is not prohibitive and when the population includes actors motivated to enforce norms even while defecting themselves. However, the findings are based on simulation and focus on the prevalence of strategies and 'level of solidarity' rather than explicit group efficiency or payoff. The results suggest that the effectiveness of punishment in raising efficiency will depend on the cost and effectiveness of sanctions, the strategic mix in the population, and the degree of inequality. The paper does not provide quantitative predictions of efficiency, so its value for direct prediction is limited to qualitative insights about when punishment is likely to be effective.

- source: Bshary, R., & Bergmueller, R. (2008). Distinguishing four fundamental approaches to the evolution of helping. *Journal of Evolutionary Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper clarifies that punishment is a key control mechanism in the evolution and maintenance of cooperation, especially in repeated game structures.
